<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> We prove that the full static displacement-to-traction map uniquely determines both real smooth Lamé moduli on every bounded connected smooth domain in ℝ<sup>3</sup>, provided <i>μ</i> &gt; 0 and $`3\lambda+2\mu\gt 0`$ on the closure. This resolves the smooth three-dimensional isotropic elastic Calderón uniqueness problem under these positivity assumptions.

- **claim-main-uniqueness** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-main)): Let $\Omega\subset\mathbb R^3$ be any bounded connected domain with $C^\infty$ boundary. For $j=1,2$, let $\lambda_j,\mu_j\in C^\infty(\overline\Omega;\mathbb R)$ satisfy $\mu_j>0$ and $3\lambda_j+2\mu_j>0$ on $\overline\Omega$. If $$\Lambda_{\lambda_1,\mu_1} =\Lambda_{\lambda_2,\mu_2} \colon H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega;\mathbb R^3) \longrightarrow H^{-1/2}(\partial\Omega;\mathbb R^3),$$ then $\lambda_1=\lambda_2$ and $\mu_1=\mu_2$ throughout $\Omega$.
  - Explanation: Equality of the full static displacement-to-traction maps of two smooth admissible Lamé pairs forces both moduli to coincide throughout the domain.
- **claim-common-extension** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-common-extension)): The two pairs admit real smooth extensions to $\mathbb R^3$ that satisfy <a href="#eq:energy-positivity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:energy-positivity">[eq:energy-positivity]</a> everywhere, coincide on $\mathbb R^3\setminus\Omega$, and equal a common constant admissible pair outside a ball.
  - Explanation: Boundary jets of the two pairs agree, so both pairs extend smoothly and admissibly to the whole space, coincide outside the domain, and are a common constant outside a ball.
- **claim-physical-transfer** (theorem, [source](#stmt-prop-physical-transfer)): Let $B$ be a ball containing $\overline B_r$. Every smooth solution $u_1$ of $L_1u_1=0$ on $B$ has a smooth counterpart $u_2$ satisfying $L_2u_2=0$ on $B$ and $$u_2=u_1\quad\text{on }B\setminus\overline\Omega.$$ In particular, $u_2-u_1$ and $\mathop{\mathrm{div}}u_2-\mathop{\mathrm{div}}u_1$ are smooth and supported in $\overline\Omega$.
  - Explanation: Every smooth solution in the first medium has a smooth counterpart in the second medium that agrees with it outside the domain; their difference and the difference of divergences are supported in the closure of the domain.
- **claim-augmented-system** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-augmented-system)): For every smooth pair $(u,d)$, $$\label{eq:augmented-identity} Lu=R(u,\mathop{\mathrm{div}}u),\qquad \mathop{\mathrm{div}}R(u,d)-S(u,d)=P(\mathop{\mathrm{div}}u-d).$$ In particular, $Lu=0$ implies $R(u,\mathop{\mathrm{div}}u)=S(u,\mathop{\mathrm{div}}u)=0$. The normalized augmented operator $$\label{eq:normalized-augmented} \mathcal LU= \left(m^{-1}R(u,d),\, \ell^{-1}\bigl(S(u,d)-2m^{-1}\nabla m\cdot R(u,d)\bigr)\right)$$ has the form $$\label{eq:laplace-principal-form} \mathcal L=\Delta\mathrm{Id}_4+\sum_{j=1}^3 A_j(x)\partial_j+A_0(x)$$ with smooth matrix coefficients. Consequently, the difference of the normalized augmented operators for the two coefficient pairs has order at most one and coefficients supported in $\overline\Omega$.
  - Explanation: Treating the divergence as an independent scalar gives an augmented system whose principal part is the Laplacian, with a compatibility identity for the defect between divergence and the scalar; differences between the two media are first order and supported in the closure of the domain.
- **claim-supported-carleman** (theorem, [source](#stmt-prop-carleman)): Let $B$ be a bounded ball. For $U\in C_c^\infty(B;\mathbb C^4)$ and all sufficiently small $h>0$, $$\label{eq:augmented-carleman} \left\lVert E_\tau^{-1}U\right\rVert_{H_h^1} \le Ch\left\lVert E_\tau^{-1}\mathcal LU\right\rVert_{H_h^{-1}}.$$
  - Explanation: A semiclassical Carleman estimate with a linear limiting weight controls compactly supported augmented fields in the first semiclassical Sobolev norm by the operator image in the negative-order norm.
- **claim-physical-solvability** (theorem, [source](#stmt-cor-physical-solvability)): For the same $B$ and $\theta$, and all sufficiently large $\tau$, every $w\in C_c^\infty(B;\mathbb C^3)$ satisfies $$\label{eq:physical-test-estimate} \left\lVert E_\tau^{-1}w\right\rVert_{L^2(B)} \le C\left\lVert E_\tau^{-1}Lw\right\rVert_{L^2(B)}.$$ Moreover, for every $f\in L^2(B;\mathbb C^3)$ there exists $z\in L^2(B;\mathbb C^3)$ satisfying, distributionally, $$\label{eq:physical-solvability} E_\tau^{-1}L(E_\tau z)=f\quad\text{in }B, \qquad \left\lVert z\right\rVert_{L^2(B)}\le C\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{L^2(B)}.$$
  - Explanation: The Carleman estimate yields an estimate for the conjugated physical operator on test functions and, by duality, solvability in the weighted space with a bound independent of the large parameter.
- **claim-scaled-interior** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-scaled-interior)): Let $B'\Subset B$. There exists $h_{B'}>0$ such that, whenever $0<h=\tau^{-1}<h_{B'}$ and $z,f\in L^2(B;\mathbb C^3)$ satisfy $E_\tau^{-1}L(E_\tau z)=f$ distributionally in $B$, $$\label{eq:scaled-interior} \sum_{j=0}^2 h^j\left\lVert \nabla^jz\right\rVert_{L^2(B')} \le C_{B'}\bigl(\left\lVert z\right\rVert_{L^2(B)} +h^2\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{L^2(B)}\bigr).$$ If $f$ is smooth, then $z$ is smooth in $B$ for each fixed $\tau$.
  - Explanation: Rescaled interior elliptic regularity bounds weighted derivatives up to second order of solutions of the conjugated system on a smaller ball, and gives smoothness for smooth right sides.
- **claim-planar-frames** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-planar-frames)): Let $I\subset\mathbb R$ be an open interval and let $A\in C^\infty(D_R^2\times I;\mathbb C^{n\times n})$. For each fixed $t\in I$, the equation $$\label{eq:planar-frame-equation} \partial_{\overline z}V=A(z,t)V$$ has a smooth invertible matrix solution on $D_R^2$. For every $R'<R$ and $t_0\in I$, there are an interval $I_0\Subset I$ containing $t_0$ and a smooth invertible solution $V$ on $D_{R'}^2\times I_0$.* *Given this $V$, a radius $R''<R'$, and $F\in C^\infty(D_{R'}^2\times I_0;\mathbb C^n)$, the equation $$\label{eq:planar-inhomogeneous} \partial_{\overline z}v=Av+F$$ has a smooth solution on $D_{R''}^2\times I_0$. If $F(\,\cdot\,,t)=0$ for $t\notin K$, where $K\Subset I_0$ is compact, the solution can be chosen with the same transverse support property. These assertions also hold with $D$ in place of $\partial_{\overline z}$, and for systems obtained by multiplying $D$ by an invertible smooth matrix.
  - Explanation: Planar Cauchy–Riemann systems admit smooth invertible matrix solutions on disks, with local smooth dependence on a transverse parameter, and inhomogeneous equations are solvable preserving transverse support.
- **claim-physical-amplitudes** (theorem, [source](#stmt-prop-physical-amplitudes)): For every nonzero null vector $\theta$ and every $t_0\in(-2r,2r)$, there exist an open interval $J\subset(-2r,2r)$ containing $t_0$ and three smooth solutions $(a_0^{\nu},b_0^{\nu})$, $1\le\nu\le3$, of <a href="#eq:constrained-transport" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:constrained-transport">[eq:constrained-transport]</a> on a neighborhood of $\overline B_{5r}$, with the following properties.* *The three columns form a frame of $\mathcal E_\theta$ at every point of $D_{2r}^2\times J$. For each $\nu$ and all sufficiently large $\tau$, there is $u_\tau^{\nu}\in C^\infty(B_{5r};\mathbb C^3)$ with $Lu_\tau^{\nu}=0$ such that, on putting $U_\tau^{\nu}=(u_\tau^{\nu},\mathop{\mathrm{div}}u_\tau^{\nu})$, $$\label{eq:physical-leading-limit} \begin{aligned} E_\tau^{-1}U_\tau^{\nu} &\longrightarrow (a_0^{\nu},b_0^{\nu}) &&\text{in }L^2(B_{4r}),\\ \left\lVert E_\tau^{-1}\nabla U_\tau^{\nu}\right\rVert_{L^2(B_{4r})} &=O(\tau). \end{aligned}$$
  - Explanation: Local transport frames of the constrained leading system are realized by exact physical solutions, with convergence of the leading displacement–divergence pair and a weighted bound on first derivatives.
- **claim-transport-normal-form** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-transport-normal-form)): For one coefficient pair, set $g=\nabla\log m$. The change of variables $$\label{eq:transport-change} p=\sqrt m\,a, \qquad s=-\frac{k}{2\sqrt m}\,b-\frac12 g\cdot p$$ is a smooth invertible map of $\mathcal E_\theta$ to itself. It transforms <a href="#eq:constrained-transport" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:constrained-transport">[eq:constrained-transport]</a> into $$\label{eq:transport-matrix} D_\theta\binom{p}{s} =M_\theta\binom{p}{s}, \qquad M_\theta= \begin{pmatrix} 0&\theta\\ Q_\theta\cdot&T_\theta \end{pmatrix} \quad\text{on }H_\theta\oplus\mathbb C,$$ where $$\label{eq:transport-coefficients} \begin{aligned} T_\theta&=D_\theta\log(k/\ell),\\ Q_\theta&=\frac12\left[ \left(\frac m\ell D_\theta\log k -\frac12\theta\cdot g\right)g -\frac m\ell D_\theta g\right]. \end{aligned}$$ In particular, the bottom-left entry in <a href="#eq:transport-matrix" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:transport-matrix">[eq:transport-matrix]</a> is the covector $p\mapsto Q_\theta\cdot p$. Both $D_\theta$ and $M_\theta$ depend linearly and holomorphically on $\theta$.
  - Explanation: An invertible change of variables puts the constrained transport system into a block form whose upper equation is common to both media and whose lower coefficients depend linearly and holomorphically on the null direction.
- **claim-supported-transport** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-supported-transport)): Let $\mathcal U\subset\mathbb R^d$ be open, let $n\geq1$, and suppose that $$\mathcal P_\rho =\gamma(\rho)(\partial_{y_1}+i\partial_{y_2}) +A(y,\rho), \qquad \rho\in\mathcal U,$$ where $\gamma$ is smooth and nonzero and $A$ is a smooth complex $n\times n$ matrix on a neighborhood of $\overline{D_{2r}^2}\times\mathcal U$. Then the following statements hold.* 1. *A distributional solution of $\mathcal P_\rho W=0$ on $D_{2r}^2$ whose support is a compact subset of that disk is zero.* 2. *For every integer $j\geq0$ and every $W\in H^{j+1}(D_{2r}^2;\mathbb C^n)$ supported in $\overline{D_r^2}$, $$\label{eq:supported-transport-estimate} \left\lVert W\right\rVert_{H^{j+1}(D_{2r}^2)} \leq C_j\left\lVert \mathcal P_\rho W\right\rVert_{H^j(D_{2r}^2)}, \qquad \mathop{\mathrm{supp}}W\subset\overline{D_r^2}.$$ The constants are locally uniform in $\rho$.* 3. *Suppose that $f(y,\rho)$ is smooth on a neighborhood of $\overline{D_{2r}^2}\times\mathcal U$. If the equation $\mathcal P_\rho W=f(\cdot,\rho)$ has an $L^2$ solution supported in $\overline{D_r^2}$ for a dense set of parameters $\rho$, then it has such a solution for every $\rho$. These solutions are unique and form a smooth function of $(y,\rho)$.* *The same conclusions apply to matrix-valued unknowns, after their entries are regarded as a vector.
  - Explanation: For planar operators of Cauchy–Riemann type with smooth parameters, compactly supported solutions of the homogeneous equation vanish, a coercive estimate holds on supported Sobolev spaces, and solutions on a dense set of parameters extend to all parameters smoothly and uniquely.
- **claim-transport-matching** (theorem, [source](#stmt-prop-transport-matching)): For the two extended coefficient pairs of Lemma <a href="#lem:common-extension" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:common-extension">2</a>, equality of the physical displacement-to-traction maps implies that there is a unique smooth field $$G(x,[\theta])\in\operatorname{End}(\mathcal E_\theta), \qquad (x,[\theta])\in\mathbb R^3\times\mathcal C,$$ satisfying, for every nonzero representative $\theta$, $$\label{eq:comparison-transport} \begin{aligned} D_\theta G&=M_{2,\theta}G-GM_{1,\theta},\\ G(x,[\theta])&=\mathrm{Id}\qquad\text{if }\|x\|>2r. \end{aligned}$$ For each fixed $x$, this field is a holomorphic section of $\operatorname{End}(\mathcal E)$ over $\mathcal C$. In fact, for each fixed direction the first equation has at most one smooth solution whose difference from the identity has compact spatial support.
  - Explanation: Equality of the physical maps yields a unique smooth comparison field between the two leading transports that is the identity far from the origin and holomorphic in the direction on the null conic.
- **claim-conic-bundle** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-conic-bundle)): The bundle $H$ has no nonzero global holomorphic sections, and $H/\mathcal N$ is holomorphically trivial. More precisely, under the identification $\mathcal C\simeq\mathbb P^1(\mathbb C)$, $$H\simeq\mathcal O(-1)^{\oplus2},\qquad \mathcal N\simeq\mathcal O(-2),$$ and their quotient sequence is $$\label{eq:conic-quotient} 0\longrightarrow\mathcal O(-2) \xrightarrow{\ (z_0,z_1)^T\ } \mathcal O(-1)^{\oplus2} \xrightarrow{\ (-z_1,z_0)\ } \mathcal O\longrightarrow0.$$ Here $[z_0:z_1]$ are homogeneous coordinates on $\mathbb P^1(\mathbb C)$.
  - Explanation: The null-plane bundle over the conic is a sum of two negative tautological line bundles, has no nonzero global holomorphic sections, and its quotient by the null line subbundle is trivial.
- **claim-comparison-rigidity** (theorem, [source](#stmt-prop-comparison-rigidity)): The comparison endomorphism of Proposition <a href="#prop:transport-matching" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:transport-matching">12</a> is $G=\mathrm{Id}$. Consequently, for every $x\in\mathbb R^3$ and every nonzero null vector $\theta$, $$\label{eq:equal-restricted-transports} M_{1,\theta}=M_{2,\theta}\quad\text{on }\mathcal E_\theta.$$
  - Explanation: The bundle vanishing results and a rank argument force the comparison field to be the identity, so the two transport matrices coincide on every fiber.
- **claim-finite-type** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-finite-type)): Let $U\subset\mathbb R^n$ be connected and open, with $n\ge2$, and let $\eta,q$ and $B_{ij}^{\ a}$ be smooth, real- or complex-valued functions on $U$ satisfying $$\label{eq:finite-type-hypothesis} \partial_i\partial_j\eta =\sum_{a=1}^n B_{ij}^{\ a}\partial_a\eta+\delta_{ij}q.$$ Then $(\nabla\eta,q)$ satisfies a homogeneous first-order system with smooth coefficients. In particular, if $\eta$ vanishes on a nonempty open subset of $U$, then $\eta=q=0$ on $U$.
  - Explanation: A Hessian-type overdetermined equation closes into a homogeneous first-order system for the gradient and the scalar, so vanishing on an open set propagates to the whole connected domain.

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-prop-physical-transfer",
        "exact_quote": "Let $B$ be a ball containing $\\overline B_r$. Every smooth solution $u_1$ of $L_1u_1=0$ on $B$ has a smooth counterpart $u_2$ satisfying $L_2u_2=0$ on $B$ and $$u_2=u_1\\quad\\text{on }B\\setminus\\overline\\Omega.$$ In particular, $u_2-u_1$ and $\\mathop{\\mathrm{div}}u_2-\\mathop{\\mathrm{div}}u_1$ are smooth and supported in $\\overline\\Omega$.",
        "source_id": "PM-372.1"
      },
      "evidence": [

          "source_id": "PM-372.1"

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-augmented-system",
        "exact_quote": "For every smooth pair $(u,d)$, $$\\label{eq:augmented-identity}\n Lu=R(u,\\mathop{\\mathrm{div}}u),\\qquad\n \\mathop{\\mathrm{div}}R(u,d)-S(u,d)=P(\\mathop{\\mathrm{div}}u-d).$$ In particular, $Lu=0$ implies $R(u,\\mathop{\\mathrm{div}}u)=S(u,\\mathop{\\mathrm{div}}u)=0$. The normalized augmented operator $$\\label{eq:normalized-augmented}\n \\mathcal LU=\n \\left(m^{-1}R(u,d),\\,\n \\ell^{-1}\\bigl(S(u,d)-2m^{-1}\\nabla m\\cdot R(u,d)\\bigr)\\right)$$ has the form $$\\label{eq:laplace-principal-form}\n \\mathcal L=\\Delta\\mathrm{Id}_4+\\sum_{j=1}^3 A_j(x)\\partial_j+A_0(x)$$ with smooth matrix coefficients. Consequently, the difference of the normalized augmented operators for the two coefficient pairs has order at most one and coefficients supported in $\\overline\\Omega$.",
        "source_id": "PM-372.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-lem-augmented-system",

The normalized augmented operator $$\\label{eq:normalized-augmented}\n \\mathcal LU=\n \\left(m^{-1}R(u,d),\\,\n \\ell^{-1}\\bigl(S(u,d)-2m^{-1}\\nabla m\\cdot R(u,d)\\bigr)\\right)$$ has the form $$\\label{eq:laplace-principal-form}\n \\mathcal L=\\Delta\\mathrm{Id}_4+\\sum_{j=1}^3 A_j(x)\\partial_j+A_0(x)$$ with smooth matrix coefficients.           "source_id": "PM-372.1"

      "exact_statement": "For every smooth pair $(u,d)$, $$\\label{eq:augmented-identity}\n Lu=R(u,\\mathop{\\mathrm{div}}u),\\qquad\n \\mathop{\\mathrm{div}}R(u,d)-S(u,d)=P(\\mathop{\\mathrm{div}}u-d).$$ In particular, $Lu=0$ implies $R(u,\\mathop{\\mathrm{div}}u)=S(u,\\mathop{\\mathrm{div}}u)=0$.     {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-cor-physical-solvability",
        "exact_quote": "For the same $B$ and $\\theta$, and all sufficiently large $\\tau$, every $w\\in C_c^\\infty(B;\\mathbb C^3)$ satisfies $$\\label{eq:physical-test-estimate}\n \\left\\lVert E_\\tau^{-1}w\\right\\rVert_{L^2(B)}\n \\le C\\left\\lVert E_\\tau^{-1}Lw\\right\\rVert_{L^2(B)}.$$ Moreover, for every $f\\in L^2(B;\\mathbb C^3)$ there exists $z\\in L^2(B;\\mathbb C^3)$ satisfying, distributionally, $$\\label{eq:physical-solvability}\n E_\\tau^{-1}L(E_\\tau z)=f\\quad\\text{in }B,\n \\qquad \\left\\lVert z\\right\\rVert_{L^2(B)}\\le C\\left\\lVert f\\right\\rVert_{L^2(B)}.$$",
        "source_id": "PM-372.1"
      },
      "evidence": [

          "anchor": "stmt-cor-physical-solvability",

          "source_id": "PM-372.1"
        }
      ],
      "exact_statement": "For the same $B$ and $\\theta$, and all sufficiently large $\\tau$, every $w\\in C_c^\\infty(B;\\mathbb C^3)$ satisfies $$\\label{eq:physical-test-estimate}\n \\left\\lVert E_\\tau^{-1}w\\right\\rVert_{L^2(B)}\n \\le C\\left\\lVert E_\\tau^{-1}Lw\\right\\rVert_{L^2(B)}.$$ Moreover, for every $f\\in L^2(B;\\mathbb C^3)$ there exists $z\\in L^2(B;\\mathbb C^3)$ satisfying, distributionally, $$\\label{eq:physical-solvability}\n E_\\tau^{-1}L(E_\\tau z)=f\\quad\\text{in }B,\n \\qquad \\left\\lVert z\\right\\rVert_{L^2(B)}\\le C\\left\\lVert f\\right\\rVert_{L^2(B)}.$$",

        "anchor": "stmt-lem-scaled-interior",
        "exact_quote": "Let $B'\\Subset B$.     {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-planar-frames",
        "exact_quote": "Let $I\\subset\\mathbb R$ be an open interval and let $A\\in C^\\infty(D_R^2\\times I;\\mathbb C^{n\\times n})$. For each fixed $t\\in I$, the equation $$\\label{eq:planar-frame-equation}\n \\partial_{\\overline z}V=A(z,t)V$$ has a smooth invertible matrix solution on $D_R^2$. For every $R'<R$ and $t_0\\in I$, there are an interval $I_0\\Subset I$ containing $t_0$ and a smooth invertible solution $V$ on $D_{R'}^2\\times I_0$.*\n\n\n*Given this $V$, a radius $R''<R'$, and $F\\in C^\\infty(D_{R'}^2\\times I_0;\\mathbb C^n)$, the equation $$\\label{eq:planar-inhomogeneous}\n \\partial_{\\overline z}v=Av+F$$ has a smooth solution on $D_{R''}^2\\times I_0$. If $F(\\,\\cdot\\,,t)=0$ for $t\\notin K$, where $K\\Subset I_0$ is compact, the solution can be chosen with the same transverse support property. These assertions also hold with $D$ in place of $\\partial_{\\overline z}$, and for systems obtained by multiplying $D$ by an invertible smooth matrix.",
        "source_id": "PM-372.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-lem-planar-frames",
          "exact_quote": "Let $I\\subset\\mathbb R$ be an open interval and let $A\\in C^\\infty(D_R^2\\times I;\\mathbb C^{n\\times n})$. For each fixed $t\\in I$, the equation $$\\label{eq:planar-frame-equation}\n \\partial_{\\overline z}V=A(z,t)V$$ has a smooth invertible matrix solution on $D_R^2$. For every $R'<R$ and $t_0\\in I$, there are an interval $I_0\\Subset I$ containing $t_0$ and a smooth invertible solution $V$ on $D_{R'}^2\\times I_0$.*\n\n\n*Given this $V$, a radius $R''<R'$, and $F\\in C^\\infty(D_{R'}^2\\times I_0;\\mathbb C^n)$, the equation $$\\label{eq:planar-inhomogeneous}\n \\partial_{\\overline z}v=Av+F$$ has a smooth solution on $D_{R''}^2\\times I_0$. If $F(\\,\\cdot\\,,t)=0$ for $t\\notin K$, where $K\\Subset I_0$ is compact, the solution can be chosen with the same transverse support property. These assertions also hold with $D$ in place of $\\partial_{\\overline z}$, and for systems obtained by multiplying $D$ by an invertible smooth matrix.",
          "source_id": "PM-372.1"
        }
      ],
      "exact_statement": "Let $I\\subset\\mathbb R$ be an open interval and let $A\\in C^\\infty(D_R^2\\times I;\\mathbb C^{n\\times n})$. For each fixed $t\\in I$, the equation $$\\label{eq:planar-frame-equation}\n \\partial_{\\overline z}V=A(z,t)V$$ has a smooth invertible matrix solution on $D_R^2$. For every $R'<R$ and $t_0\\in I$, there are an interval $I_0\\Subset I$ containing $t_0$ and a smooth invertible solution $V$ on $D_{R'}^2\\times I_0$.*\n\n\n*Given this $V$, a radius $R''<R'$, and $F\\in C^\\infty(D_{R'}^2\\times I_0;\\mathbb C^n)$, the equation $$\\label{eq:planar-inhomogeneous}\n \\partial_{\\overline z}v=Av+F$$ has a smooth solution on $D_{R''}^2\\times I_0$. If $F(\\,\\cdot\\,,t)=0$ for $t\\notin K$, where $K\\Subset I_0$ is compact, the solution can be chosen with the same transverse support property. These assertions also hold with $D$ in place of $\\partial_{\\overline z}$, and for systems obtained by multiplying $D$ by an invertible smooth matrix.",

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-prop-physical-amplitudes",
        "exact_quote": "For every nonzero null vector $\\theta$ and every $t_0\\in(-2r,2r)$, there exist an open interval $J\\subset(-2r,2r)$ containing $t_0$ and three smooth solutions $(a_0^{\\nu},b_0^{\\nu})$, $1\\le\\nu\\le3$, of <a href=\"#eq:constrained-transport\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:constrained-transport\">[eq:constrained-transport]</a> on a neighborhood of $\\overline B_{5r}$, with the following properties.*\n\n\n*The three columns form a frame of $\\mathcal E_\\theta$ at every point of $D_{2r}^2\\times J$. For each $\\nu$ and all sufficiently large $\\tau$, there is $u_\\tau^{\\nu}\\in C^\\infty(B_{5r};\\mathbb C^3)$ with $Lu_\\tau^{\\nu}=0$ such that, on putting $U_\\tau^{\\nu}=(u_\\tau^{\\nu},\\mathop{\\mathrm{div}}u_\\tau^{\\nu})$, $$\\label{eq:physical-leading-limit}\n\\begin{aligned}\n E_\\tau^{-1}U_\\tau^{\\nu}\n   &\\longrightarrow (a_0^{\\nu},b_0^{\\nu})\n                  &&\\text{in }L^2(B_{4r}),\\\\\n \\left\\lVert E_\\tau^{-1}\\nabla U_\\tau^{\\nu}\\right\\rVert_{L^2(B_{4r})}\n   &=O(\\tau).\n\\end{aligned}$$",
        "source_id": "PM-372.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-prop-physical-amplitudes",
          "exact_quote": "For every nonzero null vector $\\theta$ and every $t_0\\in(-2r,2r)$, there exist an open interval $J\\subset(-2r,2r)$ containing $t_0$ and three smooth solutions $(a_0^{\\nu},b_0^{\\nu})$, $1\\le\\nu\\le3$, of <a href=\"#eq:constrained-transport\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:constrained-transport\">[eq:constrained-transport]</a> on a neighborhood of $\\overline B_{5r}$, with the following properties.*\n\n\n*The three columns form a frame of $\\mathcal E_\\theta$ at every point of $D_{2r}^2\\times J$. For each $\\nu$ and all sufficiently large $\\tau$, there is $u_\\tau^{\\nu}\\in C^\\infty(B_{5r};\\mathbb C^3)$ with $Lu_\\tau^{\\nu}=0$ such that, on putting $U_\\tau^{\\nu}=(u_\\tau^{\\nu},\\mathop{\\mathrm{div}}u_\\tau^{\\nu})$, $$\\label{eq:physical-leading-limit}\n\\begin{aligned}\n E_\\tau^{-1}U_\\tau^{\\nu}\n   &\\longrightarrow (a_0^{\\nu},b_0^{\\nu})\n                  &&\\text{in }L^2(B_{4r}),\\\\\n \\left\\lVert E_\\tau^{-1}\\nabla U_\\tau^{\\nu}\\right\\rVert_{L^2(B_{4r})}\n   &=O(\\tau).\n\\end{aligned}$$",
          "source_id": "PM-372.1"
        }
      ],
      "exact_statement": "For every nonzero null vector $\\theta$ and every $t_0\\in(-2r,2r)$, there exist an open interval $J\\subset(-2r,2r)$ containing $t_0$ and three smooth solutions $(a_0^{\\nu},b_0^{\\nu})$, $1\\le\\nu\\le3$, of <a href=\"#eq:constrained-transport\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:constrained-transport\">[eq:constrained-transport]</a> on a neighborhood of $\\overline B_{5r}$, with the following properties.*\n\n\n*The three columns form a frame of $\\mathcal E_\\theta$ at every point of $D_{2r}^2\\times J$. For each $\\nu$ and all sufficiently large $\\tau$, there is $u_\\tau^{\\nu}\\in C^\\infty(B_{5r};\\mathbb C^3)$ with $Lu_\\tau^{\\nu}=0$ such that, on putting $U_\\tau^{\\nu}=(u_\\tau^{\\nu},\\mathop{\\mathrm{div}}u_\\tau^{\\nu})$, $$\\label{eq:physical-leading-limit}\n\\begin{aligned}\n E_\\tau^{-1}U_\\tau^{\\nu}\n   &\\longrightarrow (a_0^{\\nu},b_0^{\\nu})\n                  &&\\text{in }L^2(B_{4r}),\\\\\n \\left\\lVert E_\\tau^{-1}\\nabla U_\\tau^{\\nu}\\right\\rVert_{L^2(B_{4r})}\n   &=O(\\tau).\n\\end{aligned}$$",

<a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **shear and bulk moduli** ([source](#eq:energy-positivity)): Here $\mu$ is the shear modulus and $\lambda+2\mu/3$ is the bulk modulus.
- **displacement-to-traction map existence** ([source](#eq:physical-dn)): For each $f\in H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega;\mathbb R^3)$ there is a unique $u_f\in H^1(\Omega;\mathbb R^3)$ with $L_{\lambda,\mu}u_f=0$ and trace $f$.
- **complexification convention** ([source](#pm-h-4)): All differential identities are complexified by linearity.
- **comparison endomorphism** ([source](#sec:transport-matching)): Under this exterior agreement, define $$G=Y_2Y_1^{-1}\in\operatorname{End}(\mathcal E_\theta).$$
- **polarization bundles on the conic** ([source](#pm-h-11)): Let $H\to\mathcal C$ be the holomorphic bundle with fiber $H_\theta$, and let $\mathcal N\subset H$ be the null line subbundle with fiber $\mathbb C\theta$.

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **step-common-extension** [claim](#stmt-lem-common-extension) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-common-extension): Boundary jet determination from the maps, then cutoff interpolation with a constant admissible pair and gluing. -> common smooth admissible extensions
- **step-transfer** [claim](#stmt-prop-physical-transfer) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-physical-transfer): Solve the second Dirichlet problem, glue across the boundary, apply elliptic regularity. -> transferred exact solutions
- **step-augmented** [claim](#stmt-lem-augmented-system) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-augmented-system): Coordinate computation of the augmented identities and normalization. -> Laplacian principal part augmented operator
- **step-carleman** [claim](#stmt-prop-carleman) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-carleman): Convexified limiting Carleman weight, absorption of lower order terms, removal of the convexification, phase shift. -> supported Carleman estimate
- **step-solvability** [claim](#stmt-cor-physical-solvability) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cor-physical-solvability): Apply the estimate to the displacement with its divergence, then duality. -> weighted physical solvability
- **step-interior** [claim](#stmt-lem-scaled-interior) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-scaled-interior): Rescaling to unit balls with uniform elliptic estimates and a finite cover. -> weighted derivative bounds
- **step-planar-frames** [claim](#stmt-lem-planar-frames) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-planar-frames): Cauchy transform with Neumann series, Oka principle, parameter perturbation. -> smooth invertible frames and inhomogeneous solutions
- **step-amplitudes** [claim](#stmt-prop-physical-amplitudes) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-physical-amplitudes): Compatible high-order transport recursion followed by a physical correction. -> exact physical solutions realizing frames
- **step-normal-form** [claim](#stmt-lem-transport-normal-form) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-transport-normal-form): Change of variables and direct substitution into the leading equations. -> block transport matrices
- **step-supported-transport** [claim](#stmt-lem-supported-transport) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-supported-transport): Ellipticity, holomorphic identity theorem, compactness argument, smooth left inverse. -> uniqueness and smooth dependence of supported solutions
- **step-matching** [claim](#stmt-prop-transport-matching) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-transport-matching): Physical transfer, Carleman bound, weak limits, supported planar uniqueness, holomorphy by differentiating in the conjugate direction. -> holomorphic comparison field
- **step-conic-bundle** [claim](#stmt-lem-conic-bundle) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-conic-bundle): Rational parametrization of the conic and explicit bundle frames. -> no global sections of the polarization bundle
- **step-rigidity** [claim](#stmt-prop-comparison-rigidity) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-comparison-rigidity): Block analysis of the comparison field with bundle vanishing and a rank argument. -> comparison equals identity
- **step-finite-type** [claim](#stmt-lem-finite-type) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-finite-type): Prolongation to a closed first-order system and path-wise uniqueness for ordinary differential equations. -> vanishing propagates
- **step-main** [claim](#stmt-thm-main) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-finite-type-2): Equality of transports gives common ratios and a Hessian-type equation for the log-modulus difference, closed by the finite-type lemma. -> equality of both Lamé moduli

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **inv-global-uniqueness**: For smooth admissible Lamé pairs on a bounded connected smooth domain in three dimensions, equality of the full displacement-to-traction maps forces equality of both moduli throughout the domain.
- **inv-no-global-sections**: The null-plane bundle over the null conic carries no nonzero global holomorphic section, which is what forces the comparison endomorphism to be rigid.
- **inv-finite-type-propagation**: A Hessian-type overdetermined system with an isotropic trace term closes into a homogeneous first-order system, so vanishing on an open set propagates to the whole connected domain.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **anti-degenerate-moduli**: Apply the uniqueness conclusion to media where the shear modulus or the energy combination is not strictly positive. -> The forward problem, the normalisation of the transport variables and the Carleman solvability all rely on strict positivity; outside it the argument gives no conclusion.
- **anti-partial-data**: Use the theorem when only part of the boundary can be excited or measured. -> The proof matches physical solutions across the whole boundary using the complete operator; a partial measurement does not supply that matching.
- **anti-other-dimension**: Transfer the argument to planar or higher-dimensional elasticity. -> The rigidity step depends on the specific dimension of the transport fibre and on the bundle structure of the three-dimensional null conic.
- **anti-nonsmooth-media**: Apply the result to rough, discontinuous or damaged media. -> Smooth extensions across the boundary from matching boundary jets and the high-order transport recursion require smooth coefficients.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `not_applicable`. The main result is an existence and uniqueness theorem for coefficients of a partial differential equation, proved by asymptotic analysis, weak limits, the Oka principle and holomorphic bundle arguments on function spaces; no finite procedure with computable output is stated, so an executable oracle cannot decide it.

          "anchor": "sec:introduction",
          "exact_quote": "### Introduction\n\n\n\nLet $\\Omega\\subset\\mathbb R^3$ be a bounded connected domain with smooth boundary. For real functions $\\lambda,\\mu\\in C^\\infty(\\overline\\Omega)$ satisfying $$\\label{eq:energy-positivity}\n  \\mu>0,\\qquad 3\\lambda+2\\mu>0\n  \\quad\\text{on }\\overline\\Omega,$$ define the strain, stress, and static elasticity operator by $$\\label{eq:physical-operator}\n e(u)=\\frac{\\nabla u+(\\nabla u)^T}{2},\\qquad\n \\sigma_{\\lambda,\\mu}(u)=\\lambda(\\mathop{\\mathrm{div}}u)I+2\\mu e(u),\\qquad\n L_{\\lambda,\\mu}u=\\mathop{\\mathrm{div}}\\sigma_{\\lambda,\\mu}(u).$$ Here $\\mu$ is the shear modulus and $\\lambda+2\\mu/3$ is the bulk modulus. The inequalities in <a href=\"#eq:energy-positivity\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:energy-positivity\">[eq:energy-positivity]</a> give uniformly positive elastic energy. For each $f\\in H^{1/2}(\\partial\\Omega;\\mathbb R^3)$ there is a unique $u_f\\in H^1(\\Omega;\\mathbb R^3)$ with $L_{\\lambda,\\mu}u_f=0$ and trace $f$. The displacement-to-traction map is defined weakly by $$\\label{eq:physical-dn}\n \\langle\\Lambda_{\\lambda,\\mu}f,g\\rangle\n =\\int_\\Omega\\left[\n \\lambda(\\mathop{\\mathrm{div}}u_f)(\\mathop{\\mathrm{div}}v_g)+2\\mu e(u_f):e(v_g)\n \\right]\\mathrm dx,$$ where $v_g\\in H^1(\\Omega;\\mathbb R^3)$ has trace $g$, and $A:B=\\mathop{\\mathrm{tr}}(A^TB)$. The weak equation makes this expression independent of the extension. For smooth boundary data it is the traction $\\sigma_{\\lambda,\\mu}(u_f)n$, with $n$ the outward normal.\n\n\n<div id=\"thm:main\" class=\"theorem\">\n\n**Theorem 1**. *Let $\\Omega\\subset\\mathbb R^3$ be any bounded connected domain with $C^\\infty$ boundary. For $j=1,2$, let $\\lambda_j,\\mu_j\\in C^\\infty(\\overline\\Omega;\\mathbb R)$ satisfy $\\mu_j>0$ and $3\\lambda_j+2\\mu_j>0$ on $\\overline\\Omega$. If $$\\Lambda_{\\lambda_1,\\mu_1}\n =\\Lambda_{\\lambda_2,\\mu_2}\n \\colon H^{1/2}(\\partial\\Omega;\\mathbb R^3)\n       \\longrightarrow H^{-1/2}(\\partial\\Omega;\\mathbb R^3),$$ then $\\lambda_1=\\lambda_2$ and $\\mu_1=\\mu_2$ throughout $\\Omega$.*\n\n</div>\n\nThus Theorem <a href=\"#thm:main\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"thm:main\">1</a> gives a positive resolution of the smooth three-dimensional isotropic elastic Calderón uniqueness problem under <a href=\"#eq:energy-positivity\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:energy-positivity\">[eq:energy-positivity]</a>. It uses the full zero-frequency boundary operator in fixed Euclidean coordinates. The coefficients need not be analytic or close to constants, and their agreement near the boundary is not an additional hypothesis.",
          "source_object": "pm-h-3"
        }
      ],

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **limit-energy-positivity** (hypothesis): Both Lamé pairs must be real smooth on the closure with strictly positive shear modulus and positive energy combination there; the forward problem, the normalisation of the transport variables and the Carleman solvability all use these inequalities. Blocked use: ['Applying the uniqueness conclusion to media with vanishing or negative shear modulus', 'Applying it to media where the energy combination is not strictly positive']
- **limit-full-boundary-map** (hypothesis): The hypothesis is equality of the full displacement-to-traction map on all of the boundary, as a map between the stated trace spaces. Boundary-jet determination and the gluing of physical solutions across the boundary use the complete operator. Blocked use: ['Using the theorem when only a patch of the boundary is excited or measured', 'Using it with finitely many or noisy measurements as if the whole map were known']
- **limit-smooth-isotropic-class** (formal_scope): The theorem concerns smooth real isotropic coefficients on a bounded connected domain with smooth boundary, in the static operator defined by the strain and stress formulas of the introduction. Blocked use: ['Applying it to anisotropic or general Hookean stiffness tensors', 'Applying it to rough, discontinuous or damaged media', 'Applying it to time-dependent elastodynamics']
- **limit-common-extension-jets** (hypothesis): The common exterior extension relies on boundary determination of all jets from equal maps and on smooth gluing across the boundary; this is where smoothness of the coefficients up to the boundary is used. Blocked use: ['Reusing the extension step for finite regularity coefficients without a jet-determination result', 'Assuming agreement of the coefficients near the boundary without having derived it from the maps']
- **limit-nonconstructive-limits** (nonconstructive): Key steps are existence arguments: solvability by Hahn–Banach extension, frames by the Oka principle, and the second-medium transport columns as weak limits along subsequences. The proof gives no reconstruction procedure and no stability estimate. Blocked use: ['Extracting a reconstruction algorithm or running time from the proof', 'Claiming a stability or convergence rate for inversion from the uniqueness result']
- **limit-dimension-three-bundle** (edge_case): The rigidity step uses the three-dimensional transport space, the two-dimensional null-orthogonal planes and the bundle structure of the null conic; the argument is specific to three space dimensions. Blocked use: ['Transferring the bundle argument to planar or higher-dimensional elasticity']
- **limit-finite-type-connectedness** (hypothesis): The final propagation step needs a connected open set of dimension at least two; vanishing on an open subset spreads only through connected sets. Blocked use: ['Concluding global vanishing on disconnected sets from vanishing on one component', 'Applying the lemma in dimension one']

<a id="pm-formal"></a>
### Formal statement (Lean 4)

Corpus status: formalized in the source corpus (toolchain `leanprover/lean4:v4.34.1`, Mathlib `d13f23b723b8a846827a245b89c10fc7d3f11612`). Not re-checked here; the statements give the exact quantifiers and hypotheses, not a proof.

Scope note of family 372 (`lean/docs/372.md`, verbatim):

> The following describes the scope of the Lean formalization related to the following accompanying paper(s):
>
> - [Global Uniqueness for the Smooth Isotropic Elasticity Inverse Problem](../../preprints/Global-Uniqueness-for-the-Smooth-Isotropic-Elasticity-Inverse-Problem-September-24-2026/article.pdf)
>
> ## Scope
>
> The formalization proves global uniqueness in the three-dimensional static isotropic elasticity inverse problem. On a bounded connected smooth domain, let two pairs of smooth real Lamé moduli satisfy $\mu>0$ and $3\lambda+2\mu>0$ on the closure. If their full displacement-to-traction maps agree, then both Lamé moduli agree throughout the domain. The selected statement is uniqueness; it does not supply a reconstruction algorithm.

Comparator source `ElasticityUniqueness.lean` (3681 bytes, sha256 `c4c928a811cce5bd5bc46642e8dbf7e6ffd698856f1bf6ff12a511dddfaccb20`), verbatim:

```lean
import Mathlib

namespace OAI

/-!
# Global uniqueness for smooth isotropic elasticity

The physical Dirichlet-to-Neumann pairing acts on the variational trace
quotient. Its quotient norm is not identified here with boundary-chart
fractional Sobolev norms.
-/

noncomputable section
open MeasureTheory Set
open scoped BigOperators

namespace Elasticity

abbrev X := EuclideanSpace ℝ (Fin 3)
abbrev V := X

def coordVector (i : Fin 3) : X := EuclideanSpace.single i 1

def SmoothBoundary (Ω : Set X) : Prop :=
  ∀ x ∈ frontier Ω, ∃ e : OpenPartialHomeomorph X X,
    x ∈ e.source ∧
    ContDiffOn ℝ (⊤ : ℕ∞) e e.source ∧
    ContDiffOn ℝ (⊤ : ℕ∞) e.symm e.target ∧
    e x 0 = 0 ∧
    ∀ y ∈ e.source, y ∈ Ω ↔ 0 < e y 0

def Domain (Ω : Set X) : Prop :=
  IsOpen Ω ∧ IsConnected Ω ∧ Bornology.IsBounded Ω ∧ SmoothBoundary Ω

def SmoothUpTo (Ω : Set X) (f : X → ℝ) : Prop :=
  ∃ U : Set X, IsOpen U ∧ closure Ω ⊆ U ∧ ContDiffOn ℝ (⊤ : ℕ∞) f U

def Admissible (Ω : Set X) (lam mu : X → ℝ) : Prop :=
  SmoothUpTo Ω lam ∧ SmoothUpTo Ω mu ∧
    ∀ x ∈ closure Ω, 0 < mu x ∧ 0 < 3 * lam x + 2 * mu x

abbrev Ambient (Ω : Set X) :=
  Lp V 2 (volume.restrict Ω) × (Fin 3 → Lp V 2 (volume.restrict Ω))

def coordDeriv (f : X → V) (i : Fin 3) (x : X) : V :=
  fderiv ℝ f x (coordVector i)

def SmoothJets (Ω : Set X) : Set (Ambient Ω) :=
  {j | ∃ (f : X → V) (hf : MemLp f 2 (volume.restrict Ω))
      (hd : ∀ i, MemLp (coordDeriv f i) 2 (volume.restrict Ω)),
    ContDiff ℝ (⊤ : ℕ∞) f ∧
    j.1 = hf.toLp f ∧ j.2 = fun i => (hd i).toLp (coordDeriv f i)}

def TestJets (Ω : Set X) : Set (Ambient Ω) :=
  {j | ∃ (f : X → V) (hf : MemLp f 2 (volume.restrict Ω))
      (hd : ∀ i, MemLp (coordDeriv f i) 2 (volume.restrict Ω)),
    ContDiff ℝ (⊤ : ℕ∞) f ∧ HasCompactSupport f ∧ tsupport f ⊆ Ω ∧
    j.1 = hf.toLp f ∧ j.2 = fun i => (hd i).toLp (coordDeriv f i)}

abbrev H1 (Ω : Set X) := {j : Ambient Ω // j ∈ closure (SmoothJets Ω)}

def HasZeroTrace (Ω : Set X) (u : H1 Ω) : Prop :=
  u.val ∈ closure (TestJets Ω)

def SameTrace (Ω : Set X) (u v : H1 Ω) : Prop :=
  u.val - v.val ∈ closure (TestJets Ω)

abbrev BoundaryData (Ω : Set X) := Quot (SameTrace Ω)

def trace (Ω : Set X) (u : H1 Ω) : BoundaryData Ω := Quot.mk _ u

def div (Ω : Set X) (u : H1 Ω) (x : X) : ℝ :=
  ∑ i, (u.val.2 i x) i

def strain (Ω : Set X) (u : H1 Ω) (x : X) (i j : Fin 3) : ℝ :=
  ((u.val.2 j x) i + (u.val.2 i x) j) / 2

def energy (Ω : Set X) (lam mu : X → ℝ) (u v : H1 Ω) : ℝ :=
  ∫ x, (lam x * div Ω u x * div Ω v x +
    2 * mu x * ∑ i, ∑ j, strain Ω u x i j * strain Ω v x i j)
    ∂(volume.restrict Ω)

def WeakSolution (Ω : Set X) (lam mu : X → ℝ) (u : H1 Ω) : Prop :=
  ∀ v : H1 Ω, HasZeroTrace Ω v → energy Ω lam mu u v = 0

def dirichletSolution (Ω : Set X) (lam mu : X → ℝ)
    (f : BoundaryData Ω) : H1 Ω := by
  classical
  exact if h : ∃ u : H1 Ω, trace Ω u = f ∧ WeakSolution Ω lam mu u
  then Classical.choose h
  else f.out

def DN (Ω : Set X) (lam mu : X → ℝ) : BoundaryData Ω → BoundaryData Ω → ℝ :=
  fun f g => energy Ω lam mu (dirichletSolution Ω lam mu f) g.out

def MainClaim : Prop :=
  ∀ (Ω : Set X), Domain Ω →
  ∀ (lam₁ mu₁ lam₂ mu₂ : X → ℝ),
    Admissible Ω lam₁ mu₁ → Admissible Ω lam₂ mu₂ →
    DN Ω lam₁ mu₁ = DN Ω lam₂ mu₂ →
    (∀ x ∈ Ω, lam₁ x = lam₂ x) ∧ (∀ x ∈ Ω, mu₁ x = mu₂ x)

theorem global_uniqueness : MainClaim := by
  sorry

end Elasticity

end

end OAI
```

      "name": "SmoothBoundary",
      "statement_lean": "def SmoothBoundary (Ω : Set X) : Prop :=\n  ∀ x ∈ frontier Ω, ∃ e : OpenPartialHomeomorph X X,\n    x ∈ e.source ∧\n    ContDiffOn ℝ (⊤ : ℕ∞) e e.source ∧\n    ContDiffOn ℝ (⊤ : ℕ∞) e.symm e.target ∧\n    e x 0 = 0 ∧\n    ∀ y ∈ e.source, y ∈ Ω ↔ 0 < e y 0"
    },
    {
      "docstring": null,
      "kind": "def",
      "name": "Domain",
      "statement_lean": "def Domain (Ω : Set X) : Prop :=\n  IsOpen Ω ∧ IsConnected Ω ∧ Bornology.IsBounded Ω ∧ SmoothBoundary Ω"
    },
    {
      "docstring": null,

      "name": "SmoothUpTo",
      "statement_lean": "def SmoothUpTo (Ω : Set X) (f : X → ℝ) : Prop :=\n  ∃ U : Set X, IsOpen U ∧ closure Ω ⊆ U ∧ ContDiffOn ℝ (⊤ : ℕ∞) f U"
    },

      "docstring": null,

      "name": "Admissible",
      "statement_lean": "def Admissible (Ω : Set X) (lam mu : X → ℝ) : Prop :=\n  SmoothUpTo Ω lam ∧ SmoothUpTo Ω mu ∧\n    ∀ x ∈ closure Ω, 0 < mu x ∧ 0 < 3 * lam x + 2 * mu x"
    },
    {
      "docstring": null,

      "name": "Ambient",
      "statement_lean": "abbrev Ambient (Ω : Set X) :=\n  Lp V 2 (volume.restrict Ω) × (Fin 3 → Lp V 2 (volume.restrict Ω))"
    },
    {
      "docstring": null,
      "kind": "def",
      "name": "coordDeriv",
      "statement_lean": "def coordDeriv (f : X → V) (i : Fin 3) (x : X) : V :=\n  fderiv ℝ f x (coordVector i)"
    },
    {
      "docstring": null,
      "kind": "def",
      "name": "SmoothJets",
      "statement_lean": "def SmoothJets (Ω : Set X) : Set (Ambient Ω) :=\n  {j | ∃ (f : X → V) (hf : MemLp f 2 (volume.restrict Ω))\n      (hd : ∀ i, MemLp (coordDeriv f i) 2 (volume.restrict Ω)),\n    ContDiff ℝ (⊤ : ℕ∞) f ∧\n    j.1 = hf.toLp f ∧ j.2 = fun i => (hd i).toLp (coordDeriv f i)}"
    },
    {
      "docstring": null,
      "kind": "def",

      "statement_lean": "def TestJets (Ω : Set X) : Set (Ambient Ω) :=\n  {j | ∃ (f : X → V) (hf : MemLp f 2 (volume.restrict Ω))\n      (hd : ∀ i, MemLp (coordDeriv f i) 2 (volume.restrict Ω)),\n    ContDiff ℝ (⊤ : ℕ∞) f ∧ HasCompactSupport f ∧ tsupport f ⊆ Ω ∧\n    j.1 = hf.toLp f ∧ j.2 = fun i => (hd i).toLp (coordDeriv f i)}"
    },
    {
      "docstring": null,
      "kind": "abbrev",
      "name": "H1",
      "statement_lean": "abbrev H1 (Ω : Set X) := {j : Ambient Ω // j ∈ closure (SmoothJets Ω)}"
    },
    {
      "docstring": null,
      "kind": "def",
      "name": "HasZeroTrace",
      "statement_lean": "def HasZeroTrace (Ω : Set X) (u : H1 Ω) : Prop :=\n  u.val ∈ closure (TestJets Ω)"
    },
    {
      "docstring": null,
      "kind": "def",
      "name": "SameTrace",
      "statement_lean": "def SameTrace (Ω : Set X) (u v : H1 Ω) : Prop :=\n  u.val - v.val ∈ closure (TestJets Ω)"
    },
    {
      "docstring": null,

      "name": "BoundaryData",
      "statement_lean": "abbrev BoundaryData (Ω : Set X) := Quot (SameTrace Ω)"
    },

      "docstring": null,
      "kind": "def",
      "name": "trace",
      "statement_lean": "def trace (Ω : Set X) (u : H1 Ω) : BoundaryData Ω := Quot.mk _ u"
    },

      "docstring": null,
      "kind": "def",
      "name": "div",
      "statement_lean": "def div (Ω : Set X) (u : H1 Ω) (x : X) : ℝ :=\n  ∑ i, (u.val.2 i x) i"
    },

      "docstring": null,
      "kind": "def",
      "name": "strain",
      "statement_lean": "def strain (Ω : Set X) (u : H1 Ω) (x : X) (i j : Fin 3) : ℝ :=\n  ((u.val.2 j x) i + (u.val.2 i x) j) / 2"
    },
    {
      "docstring": null,
      "kind": "def",

      "statement_lean": "def energy (Ω : Set X) (lam mu : X → ℝ) (u v : H1 Ω) : ℝ :=\n  ∫ x, (lam x * div Ω u x * div Ω v x +\n    2 * mu x * ∑ i, ∑ j, strain Ω u x i j * strain Ω v x i j)\n    ∂(volume.restrict Ω)"
    },
    {
      "docstring": null,
      "kind": "def",
      "name": "WeakSolution",
      "statement_lean": "def WeakSolution (Ω : Set X) (lam mu : X → ℝ) (u : H1 Ω) : Prop :=\n  ∀ v : H1 Ω, HasZeroTrace Ω v → energy Ω lam mu u v = 0"
    },
    {
      "docstring": null,
      "kind": "def",
      "name": "dirichletSolution",
      "statement_lean": "def dirichletSolution (Ω : Set X) (lam mu : X → ℝ)\n    (f : BoundaryData Ω) : H1 Ω := by\n  classical\n  exact if h : ∃ u : H1 Ω, trace Ω u = f ∧ WeakSolution Ω lam mu u\n  then Classical.choose h\n  else f.out"
    },

    },

      "docstring": null,

    },

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


We prove that the full static displacement-to-traction map uniquely determines both real smooth Lamé moduli on every bounded connected smooth domain in ℝ<sup>3</sup>, provided <i>μ</i> &gt; 0 and $`3\lambda+2\mu\gt 0`$ on the closure. This resolves the smooth three-dimensional isotropic elastic Calderón uniqueness problem under these positivity assumptions.

<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### Introduction

<a id="eq:energy-positivity"></a>
<a id="eq:physical-dn"></a>
Let $\Omega\subset\mathbb R^3$ be a bounded connected domain with smooth boundary. For real functions $\lambda,\mu\in C^\infty(\overline\Omega)$ satisfying $$\label{eq:energy-positivity}
  \mu>0,\qquad 3\lambda+2\mu>0
  \quad\text{on }\overline\Omega,$$ define the strain, stress, and static elasticity operator by <a id="eq:physical-operator"></a>$$\label{eq:physical-operator}
 e(u)=\frac{\nabla u+(\nabla u)^T}{2},\qquad
 \sigma_{\lambda,\mu}(u)=\lambda(\mathop{\mathrm{div}}u)I+2\mu e(u),\qquad
 L_{\lambda,\mu}u=\mathop{\mathrm{div}}\sigma_{\lambda,\mu}(u).$$ Here $\mu$ is the shear modulus and $\lambda+2\mu/3$ is the bulk modulus. The inequalities in <a href="#eq:energy-positivity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:energy-positivity">[eq:energy-positivity]</a> give uniformly positive elastic energy. For each $f\in H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega;\mathbb R^3)$ there is a unique $u_f\in H^1(\Omega;\mathbb R^3)$ with $L_{\lambda,\mu}u_f=0$ and trace $f$. The displacement-to-traction map is defined weakly by $$\label{eq:physical-dn}
 \langle\Lambda_{\lambda,\mu}f,g\rangle
 =\int_\Omega\left[
 \lambda(\mathop{\mathrm{div}}u_f)(\mathop{\mathrm{div}}v_g)+2\mu e(u_f):e(v_g)
 \right]\mathrm dx,$$ where $v_g\in H^1(\Omega;\mathbb R^3)$ has trace $g$, and $A:B=\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(A^TB)$. The weak equation makes this expression independent of the extension. For smooth boundary data it is the traction $\sigma_{\lambda,\mu}(u_f)n$, with $n$ the outward normal.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1**. *Let $\Omega\subset\mathbb R^3$ be any bounded connected domain with $C^\infty$ boundary. For $j=1,2$, let $\lambda_j,\mu_j\in C^\infty(\overline\Omega;\mathbb R)$ satisfy $\mu_j>0$ and $3\lambda_j+2\mu_j>0$ on $\overline\Omega$. If $$\Lambda_{\lambda_1,\mu_1}
 =\Lambda_{\lambda_2,\mu_2}
 \colon H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega;\mathbb R^3)
       \longrightarrow H^{-1/2}(\partial\Omega;\mathbb R^3),$$ then $\lambda_1=\lambda_2$ and $\mu_1=\mu_2$ throughout $\Omega$.*

</div>

Thus Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> gives a positive resolution of the smooth three-dimensional isotropic elastic Calderón uniqueness problem under <a href="#eq:energy-positivity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:energy-positivity">[eq:energy-positivity]</a>. It uses the full zero-frequency boundary operator in fixed Euclidean coordinates. The coefficients need not be analytic or close to constants, and their agreement near the boundary is not an additional hypothesis.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
###### History and significance.

The inverse problem asks whether static boundary measurements determine both spatially varying elastic moduli. The scalar conductivity problem posed by Calderón (Calderón 1980) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[Calderon1980](#ref-Calderon1980)] and the complex geometric optics uniqueness theorem of Sylvester and Uhlmann (Sylvester and Uhlmann 1987) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[SylvesterUhlmann1987](#ref-SylvesterUhlmann1987)] are important antecedents, but elasticity couples two moduli through vector displacements. Nakamura and Uhlmann recovered the elastic boundary Taylor series (Nakamura and Uhlmann 1995) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[NakamuraUhlmann1995Boundary](#ref-NakamuraUhlmann1995Boundary)]. Their original global claim (Nakamura and Uhlmann 1994) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[NU1994](#ref-NU1994)] was corrected in (Nakamura and Uhlmann 2003) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[NU2003](#ref-NU2003)]. The erratum identifies two defects: the prescribed initial-value problem for a planar matrix transport need not be solvable, and substitution of the corrected complex geometric optics solutions yields a pseudodifferential equation where a partial differential equation had been asserted. Its corrected theorem proves uniqueness when both $\|\nabla\mu_j\|_{C^m}$ are sufficiently small.

Eskin and Ralston developed higher-order matrix complex geometric optics expansions using invertible planar transport frames, together with restricted elasticity uniqueness results (Eskin and Ralston 2002, 2003) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[EskinRalston2002Elasticity](#ref-EskinRalston2002Elasticity), [EskinRalston2004](#ref-EskinRalston2004)]. A direct inspection of the differential map from their auxiliary vector–scalar fields to physical displacement (Eskin and Ralston 2003, sec. 3) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[EskinRalston2004](#ref-EskinRalston2004)] shows that it is noninjective. The reduction alone therefore does not transfer equality of the physical boundary maps to equality of unrestricted auxiliary Cauchy data. The proof here transfers exact physical solutions while retaining their actual divergence.

In two dimensions, Imanuvilov and Yamamoto proved global uniqueness for smooth coefficients satisfying $\mu>0$ and $\lambda+\mu>0$, without a smallness assumption (Imanuvilov and Yamamoto 2015) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[ImanuvilovYamamoto2015Elasticity](#ref-ImanuvilovYamamoto2015Elasticity)]. In three dimensions, Lin and Nakamura gave local boundary reconstruction at finite regularity (Lin and Nakamura 2017) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[LinNakamura2017](#ref-LinNakamura2017)]; Tan and Liu obtained boundary jets on smooth Riemannian manifolds and global recovery under their analytic hypotheses (Tan and Liu 2023) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[TanLiu2023](#ref-TanLiu2023)]. A recent preprint of Chen, Jiang, Liu, and Tao treats structured shear moduli under axisymmetry, separation, or directional quasianalyticity in the specified geometries (Chen et al. 2026) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[ChenJiangLiuTao2026Elasticity](#ref-ChenJiangLiuTao2026Elasticity)]. Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> treats the full smooth class under <a href="#eq:energy-positivity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:energy-positivity">[eq:energy-positivity]</a>.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
###### Conventions.

All differential identities are complexified by linearity. Dot products of complex vectors, including the null condition and orthogonality, use the complex bilinear extension of the Euclidean product. Sobolev norms and Hilbert-space adjoints use the usual Hermitian structure. Equality of the real boundary maps extends complex linearly to the complex boundary data used in the proof.

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
###### Proof strategy.

The proof converts equality of the boundary maps into an exact exterior comparison of physical solutions. Boundary determination first gives matching boundary jets, from which we construct common smooth extensions of the coefficients outside $\Omega$. Given a solution in the first medium, equality of displacement and traction then lets us glue a solution in the second medium to it across $\partial\Omega$. The two solutions, and their actual divergences, coincide in the exterior. Section <a href="#sec:boundary-reduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:boundary-reduction">2</a> establishes this transfer.

Fix a nonzero complex null vector $\theta$, so $\theta\cdot\theta=0$, and write $D_\theta=\theta\cdot\nabla$. The real and imaginary parts of $\theta$ span a plane on which $D_\theta$ is a nonzero multiple of a Cauchy–Riemann operator; the remaining real coordinate is transverse to these planes. For solutions with exponential phase $e^{\tau\theta\cdot x}$, the leading displacement–divergence pair $(a,b)$ has $a\in H_\theta=\{a\in\mathbb C^3:\theta\cdot a=0\}$ and $b\in\mathbb C$. The resulting transport space $\mathcal E_\theta=H_\theta\oplus\mathbb C$ has dimension three. The displacement–divergence reduction to a system with Laplacian principal part is classical in Lamé unique continuation (Weck 1969; Imanuvilov and Yamamoto 2004) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[Weck1969](#ref-Weck1969), [ImanuvilovYamamoto2004Carleman](#ref-ImanuvilovYamamoto2004Carleman)]. The formulation here uses an independent vector–scalar pair $(u,d)$, retains that principal part after normalization, and satisfies a compatibility identity for the defect $\mathop{\mathrm{div}}u-d$. Its supported Carleman estimate, together with a compatible high-order transport recursion and a correction using the physical elasticity operator, realizes a full local frame in $\mathcal E_\theta$ by exact displacements. The construction controls both the leading pair and its weighted first derivatives. Sections <a href="#sec:analytic-estimates" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:analytic-estimates">3</a> and <a href="#sec:physical-amplitudes" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:physical-amplitudes">4</a> prove these facts.

The leading equations have a useful normalization. For each coefficient pair, set $$p=\sqrt\mu\,a,\qquad
 s=-\frac{\lambda+\mu}{2\sqrt\mu}\,b
       -\frac12\nabla\log\mu\cdot p.$$ This is an invertible change of variables on $\mathcal E_\theta$, since $\mu>0$ and $\lambda+\mu>0$. In the $j$th medium, the normalized transport takes the form $$D_\theta\binom{p}{s}
   =M_{j,\theta}\binom{p}{s},\qquad
 M_{j,\theta}=
 \begin{pmatrix}0&\theta\\Q_{j,\theta}\cdot&T_{j,\theta}\end{pmatrix}.$$ Here $Q_{j,\theta}\cdot$ is the covector $p\mapsto Q_{j,\theta}\cdot p$. The lower coefficients are smooth in $x$ and linear in $\theta$; Lemma <a href="#lem:transport-normal-form" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:transport-normal-form">10</a> gives their formulas. The upper equation $D_\theta p=\theta s$ is the same for both media.

Transport comparison followed by holomorphic variation of the complex direction has a close precedent in Cekić’s work on connection Laplacians (Cekić 2025) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[Cekic2025](#ref-Cekic2025)], whose parameter argument follows Eskin’s analysis of Yang–Mills potentials (Eskin 2001) <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[Eskin2001](#ref-Eskin2001)]. Cekić treats full connection-system boundary data on a trivial vector bundle. The comparison needed here must be obtained from physical elasticity data on the constrained fibers $\mathcal E_\theta$.

On a cylinder given by a planar disk times a transverse interval, let $Y_1:\mathbb C^3\to\mathcal E_\theta$ be the matrix of normalized columns of one local frame realized in the first medium. Transfer its exact physical solutions to the second medium. The two augmented operators have the same principal part, so the forcing for the difference of the physical pairs has only first order. The derivative bound and the supported Carleman estimate make the transferred leading pairs bounded in $L^2$. Weak limits supply a matrix $Y_2:\mathbb C^3\to\mathcal E_\theta$ of three second-medium transport columns. The two normalizations agree in the exterior because the coefficients do, so $Y_2=Y_1$ there. The local comparison $G=Y_2Y_1^{-1}$ is therefore an endomorphism of $\mathcal E_\theta$ equal to the identity on the part of that cylinder outside $\overline\Omega$. Only the original columns $Y_1$ need to form an invertible frame.

The initial comparison is obtained in a weak sense on transverse cylinders. Section <a href="#sec:transport-matching" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:transport-matching">5</a> proves uniqueness for supported planar transport equations and an estimate on fixed supported Sobolev spaces. These results assemble the local comparisons into a unique smooth field $G(x,[\theta])\in\operatorname{End}(\mathcal E_\theta)$ on $\mathbb R^3\times\mathcal C$, where $\mathcal C=\{[\theta]\in\mathbb C\mathrm P^2:\theta\cdot\theta=0\}$ is the projective null conic. For a fixed ball $\mathcal B\subset\mathbb R^3$ independent of $[\theta]$, it satisfies $$D_\theta G=M_{2,\theta}G-GM_{1,\theta},
 \qquad G(x,[\theta])=\mathrm{Id}\quad(x\notin\mathcal B).$$ Differentiating this equation in a conjugate conic coordinate while holding $x$ fixed gives a supported homogeneous transport equation. Its uniqueness makes $G(x,\cdot)$ holomorphic.

Finally, the planes $H_\theta$ form a holomorphic bundle $H$ on $\mathcal C$. It has no nonzero global holomorphic sections, while its quotient by the null line subbundle with fiber $\mathbb C\theta$ is trivial. These facts and the universal upper transport equation force the displacement block of $G$ to be scalar. The remaining upper block equation uses $\dim H_\theta=2$ to make that scalar constant in $x$ and remove the remaining off-diagonal block; the exterior value then gives $G=\mathrm{Id}$. Equality of the transports first determines $(\lambda+\mu)/(\lambda+2\mu)$. The remaining equations for $\eta=\log\mu_1-\log\mu_2$ have the form $\nabla^2\eta=B(x)\nabla\eta+q(x)I$, where $B(x):\mathbb R^3\to\mathbb R^{3\times3}$ and the scalar $q(x)$ are smooth. One differentiation closes a homogeneous first-order system for $(\nabla\eta,q)$, whose zero exterior values propagate by uniqueness for ordinary differential equations along paths. Section <a href="#sec:coefficient-recovery" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:coefficient-recovery">6</a> gives this rigidity and recovers both coefficients.

<a id="sec:boundary-reduction"></a>
### Boundary reduction and physical transfer

Throughout the proof, the two coefficient pairs satisfy the hypotheses of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> and have equal displacement-to-traction maps.

<a id="stmt-lem-common-extension"></a>
<div id="lem:common-extension" class="lemma">

**Lemma 2** (Common exterior extension). *The two pairs admit real smooth extensions to $\mathbb R^3$ that satisfy <a href="#eq:energy-positivity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:energy-positivity">[eq:energy-positivity]</a> everywhere, coincide on $\mathbb R^3\setminus\Omega$, and equal a common constant admissible pair outside a ball.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-common-extension"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-common-extension</summary>

*Proof.* By Theorem 1.2 of Tan and Liu (Tan and Liu 2023) <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[TanLiu2023](#ref-TanLiu2023)], equality of the elastic Dirichlet-to-Neumann maps determines all boundary partial derivatives of both Lamé moduli. Our inequalities imply $\lambda+\mu>\mu/3>0$, so their positivity condition holds. Their definition of the boundary operator is $\lambda(\mathop{\mathrm{div}}u)n+\mu(\nabla u+(\nabla u)^T)n$, so its Euclidean specialization agrees with <a href="#eq:physical-dn" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:physical-dn">[eq:physical-dn]</a>. Consequently the two pairs have identical jets at every point of $\partial\Omega$. Lin and Nakamura also give local boundary reconstruction, with curved boundaries discussed in Section 4 of (Lin and Nakamura 2017) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[LinNakamura2017](#ref-LinNakamura2017)].

Smoothly extend the first pair to a neighborhood of $\overline\Omega$. By compactness and strict positivity, shrink the neighborhood so that <a href="#eq:energy-positivity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:energy-positivity">[eq:energy-positivity]</a> still holds there. Choose a smooth cutoff equal to one on a smaller neighborhood of $\overline\Omega$ and supported in the first neighborhood. Interpolate with any constant pair satisfying <a href="#eq:energy-positivity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:energy-positivity">[eq:energy-positivity]</a>. The admissible set in the $(\lambda,\mu)$ plane is convex, so this produces a smooth admissible global extension of the first pair, constant outside a compact set.

For the second pair use its prescribed values in $\Omega$ and the first extension on the complement. Equality of all jets makes this piecewise definition smooth across each boundary component. Positivity holds on both sides and on the boundary. This proves the assertion, including when the complement of $\Omega$ has bounded components. ◻

</details>

Fix these extensions and retain the notation $\lambda_j,\mu_j$ and $L_j=L_{\lambda_j,\mu_j}$ for them. Choose $r>0$ so that $\overline\Omega\subset B_r$ and the extensions are constant outside $B_r$. Here $B_R$ is the open ball of radius $R$ centered at the origin.

<a id="stmt-prop-physical-transfer"></a>
<div id="prop:physical-transfer" class="proposition">

**Proposition 3** (Transfer of physical solutions). *Let $B$ be a ball containing $\overline B_r$. Every smooth solution $u_1$ of $L_1u_1=0$ on $B$ has a smooth counterpart $u_2$ satisfying $L_2u_2=0$ on $B$ and $$u_2=u_1\quad\text{on }B\setminus\overline\Omega.$$ In particular, $u_2-u_1$ and $\mathop{\mathrm{div}}u_2-\mathop{\mathrm{div}}u_1$ are smooth and supported in $\overline\Omega$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-physical-transfer"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-physical-transfer</summary>

*Proof.* Solve the second Dirichlet problem in $\Omega$ with trace $u_1|_{\partial\Omega}$, and use $u_1$ outside $\overline\Omega$. Matching traces give an $H^1_{\mathrm{loc}}(B)$ field $u_2$. The restrictions of the two interior solutions have identical boundary tractions because their Dirichlet values and boundary maps agree. For a smooth test vector compactly supported in $B$, the weak boundary term contributed by the interior therefore agrees with that for $u_1$. The coefficients and displacements coincide on the exterior, where the same test contributes the opposite interface term. Hence the glued field satisfies $L_2u_2=0$ weakly on $B$.

The extended operator is smooth and strongly elliptic. Interior elliptic regularity, applied also across $\partial\Omega$, gives $u_2\in C^\infty(B;\mathbb C^3)$. The difference is zero off $\overline\Omega$, as are all its derivatives there. Its support and the support of its divergence are thus contained in $\overline\Omega\Subset B_r$. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:analytic-estimates"></a>
### An augmented system and analytic estimates

<a id="eq:augmented-operators"></a>
This section constructs a scalar-Laplacian augmented operator and a supported estimate that will control differences of transferred physical displacement–divergence pairs. Adjoining the displacement divergence to obtain a system with Laplacian principal part goes back to Weck (Weck 1969) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[Weck1969](#ref-Weck1969)]; see the account of Imanuvilov and Yamamoto (Imanuvilov and Yamamoto 2004, sec. 1) <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[ImanuvilovYamamoto2004Carleman](#ref-ImanuvilovYamamoto2004Carleman)]. The identities below give the formulation used in this proof. We first work with one of the smoothly extended coefficient pairs from Lemma <a href="#lem:common-extension" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:common-extension">2</a>, and suppress its subscript. Set $$m=\mu,\qquad k=\lambda+\mu,\qquad \ell=\lambda+2\mu=k+m.$$ The energy assumptions imply $m>0$, $k>m/3$, and $\ell>4m/3$. For an independent vector–scalar pair $U=(u,d)$, define $$\label{eq:augmented-operators}
\begin{aligned}
 R(u,d)_i
  & =m\Delta u_i+k\partial_i d
   +\sum_{j=1}^3(\partial_jm)(\partial_ju_i+\partial_iu_j)
   +(\partial_i\lambda)d,\\
 S(u,d)
  & =\ell\Delta d+2\nabla k\cdot\nabla d
   +2\nabla m\cdot\Delta u
   +2\sum_{i,j=1}^3(\partial_i\partial_jm)\partial_iu_j
   +(\Delta\lambda)d,\\
 P&=m\Delta+\nabla m\cdot\nabla.
\end{aligned}$$ The scalar $d$ will equal $\mathop{\mathrm{div}}u$ for physical solutions, but keeping it independent gives the compatibility identity needed below.

<a id="stmt-lem-augmented-system"></a>
<div id="lem:augmented-system" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:augmented-identity"></a>
<a id="eq:normalized-augmented"></a>
<a id="eq:laplace-principal-form"></a>
**Lemma 4**. *For every smooth pair $(u,d)$, $$\label{eq:augmented-identity}
 Lu=R(u,\mathop{\mathrm{div}}u),\qquad
 \mathop{\mathrm{div}}R(u,d)-S(u,d)=P(\mathop{\mathrm{div}}u-d).$$ In particular, $Lu=0$ implies $R(u,\mathop{\mathrm{div}}u)=S(u,\mathop{\mathrm{div}}u)=0$. The normalized augmented operator $$\label{eq:normalized-augmented}
 \mathcal LU=
 \left(m^{-1}R(u,d),\,
 \ell^{-1}\bigl(S(u,d)-2m^{-1}\nabla m\cdot R(u,d)\bigr)\right)$$ has the form $$\label{eq:laplace-principal-form}
 \mathcal L=\Delta\mathrm{Id}_4+\sum_{j=1}^3 A_j(x)\partial_j+A_0(x)$$ with smooth matrix coefficients. Consequently, the difference of the normalized augmented operators for the two coefficient pairs has order at most one and coefficients supported in $\overline\Omega$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-augmented-system"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-augmented-system</summary>

*Proof.* The first identity is the coordinate expansion of $\mathop{\mathrm{div}}\sigma(u)$. To verify the second independently of the constraint, write $v=\mathop{\mathrm{div}}u$. Differentiating <a href="#eq:augmented-operators" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:augmented-operators">[eq:augmented-operators]</a> gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathop{\mathrm{div}}R(u,d)
 ={}&m\Delta v+\nabla m\cdot\nabla v+k\Delta d
       +(\nabla k+\nabla\lambda)\cdot\nabla d\\
    &+2\nabla m\cdot\Delta u
       +2\sum_{i,j=1}^3(\partial_i\partial_jm)\partial_iu_j
       +(\Delta\lambda)d.
\end{aligned}$$ Subtracting $S$ and using $k=\lambda+m$ gives $m\Delta(v-d)+\nabla m\cdot\nabla(v-d)$.

For the principal part in <a href="#eq:normalized-augmented" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:normalized-augmented">[eq:normalized-augmented]</a>, the only second derivatives of $u$ in its scalar numerator cancel. More explicitly, if $$C_m(u)_i=\sum_{j=1}^3(\partial_jm)
                   (\partial_ju_i+\partial_iu_j),$$ then this numerator is $$\begin{aligned}
 S-2m^{-1}\nabla m\cdot R
 ={}&\ell\Delta d+
   (2\nabla k-2km^{-1}\nabla m)\cdot\nabla d\\
 &+2\sum_{i,j=1}^3(\partial_i\partial_jm)\partial_iu_j
   -2m^{-1}\nabla m\cdot C_m(u)\\
 &+\bigl(\Delta\lambda-2m^{-1}\nabla m\cdot\nabla\lambda\bigr)d.
\end{aligned}$$ This proves <a href="#eq:laplace-principal-form" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:laplace-principal-form">[eq:laplace-principal-form]</a>. The support assertion follows from equality of the extended coefficients off $\overline\Omega$. ◻

</details>

The order-one difference of the two augmented operators is the forcing term to be estimated after physical transfer in Section <a href="#sec:transport-matching" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:transport-matching">5</a>.

<a id="eq:phase-notation"></a>
Fix a nonzero $\theta\in\mathbb C^3$ with $\theta\cdot\theta=0$, and write $$\label{eq:phase-notation}
 D_\theta=\theta\cdot\nabla,\qquad
 E_\tau(x)=e^{\tau\theta\cdot x},\qquad h=\tau^{-1}.$$ For $s\in\mathbb R$, the semiclassical Sobolev norm on $\mathbb R^3$ is $$\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{H_h^s}^2
 =\int_{\mathbb R^3}(1+h^2\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert^2)^s
                    \left\lvert \widehat v(\xi)\right\rvert^2\,\mathrm d\xi.$$ For vectors, we sum the component norms. These norms and all Hilbert space adjoints below use the Hermitian structure, whereas products such as $\theta\cdot\theta$ remain complex bilinear. Constants in the following estimates may depend on the coefficients, the fixed ball, and $\theta$, but not on sufficiently large $\tau$.

<a id="stmt-prop-carleman"></a>
<div id="prop:carleman" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:augmented-carleman"></a>
**Proposition 5** (Supported Carleman estimate). *Let $B$ be a bounded ball. For $U\in C_c^\infty(B;\mathbb C^4)$ and all sufficiently small $h>0$, $$\label{eq:augmented-carleman}
 \left\lVert E_\tau^{-1}U\right\rVert_{H_h^1}
 \le Ch\left\lVert E_\tau^{-1}\mathcal LU\right\rVert_{H_h^{-1}}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-carleman"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-carleman</summary>

<a id="eq:convexified-carleman"></a>
*Proof.* Put $\varphi(x)=-\operatorname{Re}(\theta\cdot x)$. A nonzero complex null vector has nonzero real part, so $\varphi$ is a nonconstant linear limiting Carleman weight for the Laplacian. For $\varphi_\varepsilon=\varphi+h\varphi^2/(2\varepsilon)$, (Salo and Tzou 2009, Lemma 2.1), with Sobolev index $s=-1$, gives $$\label{eq:convexified-carleman}
 \frac{h}{\sqrt\varepsilon}\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{H_h^1}
 \le C\left\lVert e^{\varphi_\varepsilon/h}h^2\Delta
                 e^{-\varphi_\varepsilon/h}v\right\rVert_{H_h^{-1}},
 \qquad v\in C_c^\infty(B),$$ for $h\ll\varepsilon\ll1$, with $C$ independent of sufficiently small $\varepsilon$. Its statement has a gain of two derivatives and permits every real $s$. The sign of the Laplacian is immaterial here.

Apply <a href="#eq:convexified-carleman" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:convexified-carleman">[eq:convexified-carleman]</a> componentwise. A first-order term in <a href="#eq:laplace-principal-form" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:laplace-principal-form">[eq:laplace-principal-form]</a> contributes $$e^{\varphi_\varepsilon/h}h^2A_j\partial_j
       e^{-\varphi_\varepsilon/h}v
 =hA_j(h\partial_jv)-hA_j(\partial_j\varphi_\varepsilon)v.$$ Its $H_h^{-1}$ norm is bounded by $C_1h\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{H_h^1}$, uniformly when $h/\varepsilon$ is small. The zeroth-order terms have the same bound. Choose $\varepsilon>0$ so small that these terms are absorbed by the left side of <a href="#eq:convexified-carleman" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:convexified-carleman">[eq:convexified-carleman]</a>, and then choose $h$ sufficiently small relative to this fixed $\varepsilon$. We obtain $$\left\lVert e^{\varphi_\varepsilon/h}U\right\rVert_{H_h^1}
 \le C_\varepsilon h
       \left\lVert e^{\varphi_\varepsilon/h}\mathcal LU\right\rVert_{H_h^{-1}}.$$

The factor $e^{\varphi^2/(2\varepsilon)}$ is now fixed. It and its inverse, smoothly localized around $\overline B$, are bounded multipliers on $H_h^1$, uniformly for $0<h\le1$, by the product rule. They are bounded on $H_h^{-1}$ by duality. Removing this factor gives the same estimate with $\varphi$ in place of $\varphi_\varepsilon$. The multiplier constants may depend on $\varepsilon$; no subsequent absorption involves them.

Finally, write $\beta=\operatorname{Im}\theta$. Multiplication by $e^{-i\beta\cdot x/h}$ shifts the semiclassical frequency $h\xi$ by the fixed vector $-\beta$. The weights $1+\left\lvert h\xi\right\rvert^2$ and $1+\left\lvert h\xi-\beta\right\rvert^2$ are comparable, so this multiplier and its inverse are uniformly bounded on $H_h^{\pm1}$. Since $E_\tau^{-1}=e^{\varphi/h}e^{-i\beta\cdot x/h}$, this proves <a href="#eq:augmented-carleman" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:augmented-carleman">[eq:augmented-carleman]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-cor-physical-solvability"></a>
<div id="cor:physical-solvability" class="corollary">

<a id="eq:physical-test-estimate"></a>
<a id="eq:physical-solvability"></a>
**Corollary 6**. *For the same $B$ and $\theta$, and all sufficiently large $\tau$, every $w\in C_c^\infty(B;\mathbb C^3)$ satisfies $$\label{eq:physical-test-estimate}
 \left\lVert E_\tau^{-1}w\right\rVert_{L^2(B)}
 \le C\left\lVert E_\tau^{-1}Lw\right\rVert_{L^2(B)}.$$ Moreover, for every $f\in L^2(B;\mathbb C^3)$ there exists $z\in L^2(B;\mathbb C^3)$ satisfying, distributionally, $$\label{eq:physical-solvability}
 E_\tau^{-1}L(E_\tau z)=f\quad\text{in }B,
 \qquad \left\lVert z\right\rVert_{L^2(B)}\le C\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{L^2(B)}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-physical-solvability"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-physical-solvability</summary>

*Proof.* Apply Proposition <a href="#prop:carleman" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:carleman">5</a> to $(w,\mathop{\mathrm{div}}w)$. By <a href="#eq:augmented-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:augmented-identity">[eq:augmented-identity]</a>, its augmented forcing is formed from $Lw$ and $\mathop{\mathrm{div}}(Lw)$. If $F=E_\tau^{-1}Lw$, then $$E_\tau^{-1}\mathop{\mathrm{div}}(Lw)=\mathop{\mathrm{div}}F+\tau\theta\cdot F.$$ The Fourier definition of the norms gives $\left\lVert \partial_jF\right\rVert_{H_h^{-1}}\le h^{-1}\left\lVert F\right\rVert_{L^2}$. Smooth coefficient multipliers, localized near $\overline B$, are uniformly bounded on $H_h^{-1}$. Hence $$\left\lVert E_\tau^{-1}\mathcal L(w,\mathop{\mathrm{div}}w)\right\rVert_{H_h^{-1}}
 \le Ch^{-1}\left\lVert F\right\rVert_{L^2}.$$ Taking the displacement component on the left side of <a href="#eq:augmented-carleman" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:augmented-carleman">[eq:augmented-carleman]</a> proves <a href="#eq:physical-test-estimate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:physical-test-estimate">[eq:physical-test-estimate]</a>.

Let $A_\theta=E_\tau^{-1}LE_\tau$ on test functions in $B$. Substituting $w=E_\tau v$ in <a href="#eq:physical-test-estimate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:physical-test-estimate">[eq:physical-test-estimate]</a> gives $\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{L^2}\le C\left\lVert A_\theta v\right\rVert_{L^2}$. Formal self-adjointness of $L$ gives $$A_\theta^*
 =e^{\tau\overline\theta\cdot x}L
                      e^{-\tau\overline\theta\cdot x}
 =A_{-\overline\theta}.$$ The same estimate for the null direction $-\overline\theta$ thus holds for $A_\theta^*$. Use the $L^2$ inner product linear in its first argument. The functional $$A_\theta^*v\longmapsto (v,f)_{L^2(B)},
 \qquad v\in C_c^\infty(B;\mathbb C^3),$$ is well-defined and has norm at most $C\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{L^2}$. Extend it by the Hahn–Banach Theorem and represent it as $(\,\cdot\,,z)_{L^2}$. Then $(A_\theta^*v,z)=(v,f)$ for every test function $v$, which is the distributional equation in <a href="#eq:physical-solvability" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:physical-solvability">[eq:physical-solvability]</a>. The norm of the representing vector gives the asserted bound. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lem-scaled-interior"></a>
<div id="lem:scaled-interior" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:scaled-interior"></a>
**Lemma 7** (Scaled interior estimate). *Let $B'\Subset B$. There exists $h_{B'}>0$ such that, whenever $0<h=\tau^{-1}<h_{B'}$ and $z,f\in L^2(B;\mathbb C^3)$ satisfy $E_\tau^{-1}L(E_\tau z)=f$ distributionally in $B$, $$\label{eq:scaled-interior}
 \sum_{j=0}^2 h^j\left\lVert \nabla^jz\right\rVert_{L^2(B')}
 \le C_{B'}\bigl(\left\lVert z\right\rVert_{L^2(B)}
                         +h^2\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{L^2(B)}\bigr).$$ If $f$ is smooth, then $z$ is smooth in $B$ for each fixed $\tau$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-scaled-interior"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-scaled-interior</summary>

*Proof.* The positive principal coefficient form of $L$ is $$m\left\lvert \xi\right\rvert^2\left\lvert v\right\rvert^2+k\left\lvert \xi\cdot v\right\rvert^2,
 \qquad \xi\in\mathbb R^3,\quad v\in\mathbb C^3.$$ Thus its ellipticity is uniform on $\overline B$. Conjugation leaves the principal part unchanged and introduces coefficients of size $O(h^{-1})$ and $O(h^{-2})$ in orders one and zero. On a ball $B(x_0,2h)\subset B$, set $x=x_0+hy$ and multiply the equation by $h^2$. The resulting operator on the fixed ball $B(0,2)$ has a uniformly strongly elliptic principal part, while its coefficients and their derivatives in $y$ are uniformly bounded. As $(x_0,h)$ ranges over $\overline B'\times[0,h_0]$, these operators form a compact smooth coefficient family with a common ellipticity bound. The local parametrix estimate persists under a small coefficient perturbation, so a finite cover gives a uniform constant. Interior $H^2$ regularity for distributional $L^2$ solutions of smooth elliptic systems is given by (Dyatlov 2026, Theorem 15.1 and Remark 15.2) <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[Dyatlov2026](#ref-Dyatlov2026)]. The same parametrix argument with matrix symbols gives the localized estimate for systems corresponding to the scalar Proposition 15.6 of that source. With the uniform constants just obtained, it yields $$\sum_{j=0}^2 h^j\left\lVert \nabla^jz\right\rVert_{L^2(B(x_0,h))}
 \le C\bigl(\left\lVert z\right\rVert_{L^2(B(x_0,2h))}
                      +h^2\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{L^2(B(x_0,2h))}\bigr).$$ For small $h$, cover $B'$ by such inner balls with uniformly bounded overlap of the doubled balls. Squaring and summing proves <a href="#eq:scaled-interior" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:scaled-interior">[eq:scaled-interior]</a>. Iterated interior elliptic regularity, with $\tau$ fixed, proves the final assertion. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:physical-amplitudes"></a>
### Physical solutions realizing local transport frames

<a id="eq:leading-operators"></a>
<a id="eq:constrained-transport"></a>
<a id="eq:normal-transport-component"></a>
We now construct exact elasticity solutions with controlled leading behavior for both the displacement and its actual divergence. We begin by identifying the equations obeyed by these leading terms. Fix the null vector $\theta$ from <a href="#eq:phase-notation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:phase-notation">[eq:phase-notation]</a>, and write $D=D_\theta$ when convenient. Since $\theta\cdot\theta=0$, conjugating the operators in <a href="#eq:augmented-operators" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:augmented-operators">[eq:augmented-operators]</a> gives $$E_\tau^{-1}R(E_\tau a,E_\tau b)=R(a,b)+\tau R^\theta(a,b),
 \qquad
